=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is a grid consisting of N cells numbered 1 to N.
Initially, cell i (1 \le i \le N) has an integer i \bmod 2 written in it. You can perform the following operation any number of times, possibly zero:

- Choose cells l and r (l+1 < r) that satisfy the following conditions, and replace each of the integers written in cells l+1, l+2, \dots, r-1 with the integer written in cell l.
- The integer written in cell l is equal to the integer written in cell r.
- The integer written in cell i (l < i < r) is different from the integer written in cell l.



Find the number, modulo 998244353, of sequences of operations that result in the integers written in cell i (1 \leq i \leq N) being A_i.
Two sequences of operations are considered different if and only if their lengths are different or there exists a positive integer t not exceeding the length of the sequences such that the (l, r) chosen in the t-th operations differ.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 0 \leq A_i \leq 1

Sample Input 1

6
1 1 1 1 1 0

Sample Output 1

3

To make the integers written in each cell i equal to A_i, for example, you can perform the following operations. (Here, we represent the state of the grid as a sequence X = (X_1, X_2, \dots, X_N).)

- Initially, X = (1, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0).
- Choose cells 2 and 4. X becomes (1, 0, 0, 0, 1, 0).
- Choose cells 1 and 5. X becomes (1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 0).

Besides the above, there are two other sequences of operations that result in the integers written in cell i being A_i, so the answer is 3.

Sample Input 2

10
1 1 1 1 1 0 1 1 1 0

Sample Output 2

9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Now, let's think of another possible approach: Could operations be counted by Cartesian tree? But our formula seems solid.

Let's derive f(s) more rigorously to avoid off-by-one.

Define g(n) = number of sequences to reduce 2n+1 singleton runs to 1 run. We claim g(n) = (2n-1)!!.

Base n=0: g(0)=1 (no operations). n=1: g(1)=1.

Recurrence by last operation:
Before last operation, there are 3 runs with lengths 2i+1, 2j+1, 2k+1 where i+j+k = n-1. The number of ways to reach that state:
- Each of the three runs can be reduced independently: g(i) g(j) g(k).
- The operations from the three subproblems can be interleaved arbitrarily: total operations before last = i+j+k = n-1, so multinomial (n-1)!/(i!j!k!).
Then last operation forced. Thus
g(n) = sum_{i+j+k=n-1} (n-1)!/(i!j!k!) g(i)g(j)g(k).
Now prove g(n)=(2n-1)!! by induction using identity:
∑_{i+j+k=n-1} (n-1)!/(i!j!k!) (2i-1)!!(2j-1)!!(2k-1)!! = (2n-1)!!.

Let's prove identity. Let h(n) = (2n-1)!!. Consider exponential generating function? h(n) = (2n)!/(2^n n!). The EGF H(x)=∑_{n≥0} h(n) x^n/n! = ∑ (2n)!/(2^n n!^2) x^n? That's (1-x)^-1/2? Let's compute: ∑_{n≥0} (2n)!/(2^n n!^2) x^n = (1-x)^-1/2? Since (1-x)^-1/2 = ∑ binom(2n,n)(x/4)^n = ∑ (2n)!/(n!^2) x^n/4^n = ∑ (2n)!/(2^n n!^2) (x/2)^n? Hmm not exactly. Let's derive: h(n) = (2n)!/(2^n n!). Then h(n)/n! = (2n)!/(2^n n!^2). EGF ∑ h(n) x^n/n! = ∑ (2n)!/(2^n n!^2) x^n = ∑ binom(2n,n) (x/2)^n /? binom(2n,n)= (2n)!/(n!^2). So term = binom(2n,n) (x/2)^n? Since divide by 2^n. ∑ binom(2n,n) (x/2)^n = (1 - 4*(x/2))^-1/2 = (1-2x)^-1/2. Yes H(x)=(1-2x)^-1/2.

Now recurrence convolution with multinomial: g(n) = n! [x^n]? Actually ∑_{i+j+k=n-1} (n-1)!/(i!j!k!) g(i)g(j)g(k) = (n-1)! [x^{n-1}] (∑ g(i)x^i/i!)^3. Let G(x)=∑ g(n)x^n/n!. Then recurrence says for n≥1, g(n)/(n-1)! = [x^{n-1}] G(x)^3. Equivalently derivative? G'(x)=∑_{n≥1} g(n) x^{n-1}/(n-1)! = G(x)^3. With G(0)=1. Solve G' = G^3 => d(G^{-2}) = -2 dx? G^{-2} = 1 - 2x => G=(1-2x)^-1/2. Thus g(n) = (2n-1)!!. Nice. Good.

So f formula correct.

Now, let's consider modulo and factorial precomputation. N up to 2e5, K up to (N-1)/2 ~1e5. Need fact up to N maybe for K and invfact ops. We can precompute fact and invfact up to N (or K). Since ops_k <= K. Use MOD prime. Simpler:
fact = [1]*(N+1)
for i in 1..N: fact[i]=fact[i-1]*i%MOD
invfact[N]=pow(fact[N], MOD-2, MOD)
for i down: invfact[i-1]=invfact[i]*i%MOD
Then double_fact = [1]*(N+1)
for s in range(3,N+1,2): double_fact[s] = double_fact[s-2]*(s-2)%MOD
But note for s=1, double_fact[1]=1. For s=3, double_fact[3]=double_fact[1]*1=1. For s=5, *3=3. Good.

Answer:
ans = fact[K]
for each run length L:
  ops = (L-1)//2
  ans = ans * double_fact[L] % MOD
  ans = ans * invfact[ops] % MOD
Because fact[K] * ∏ invfact[ops].
Alternatively ans = fact[K] * ∏ (double_fact[L] * invfact[ops]) mod.

Need parse A. Input line has N then A_1..A_N. Could be all on one line but use sys.stdin.buffer.read().split().

Implementation:
import sys
MOD=998244353
def main():
 data=list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
 if not data: return
 N=data[0]; A=data[1:1+N]
 if A[0]!=1:
   print(0); return
 # parse runs and check odd
 runs=[]
 cnt=1
 for i in range(1,N):
   if A[i]==A[i-1]:
     cnt+=1
   else:
     if cnt%2==0:
       print(0); return
     runs.append(cnt)
     cnt=1
 if cnt%2==0: print(0); return
 runs.append(cnt)
 # optional check A[-1]==N%2? Not needed but can include for safety.
 # Also maybe if A[0]==1 but runs odd, A[-1] automatically N%2. But if input malformed? It's binary. No need.
 # Precompute fact/invfact up to N maybe K = sum ops.
 K = sum((L-1)//2 for L in runs)
 # Could K = (N-M)//2. If K maybe 0.
 fact=[1]*(N+1)
 for i in range(1,N+1): fact[i]=fact[i-1]*i%MOD
 invfact=[1]*(N+1)
 invfact[N]=pow(fact[N], MOD-2, MOD)
 for i in range(N,0,-1): invfact[i-1]=invfact[i]*i%MOD
 df=[1]*(N+1)
 for s in range(3,N+1,2):
    df[s]=df[s-2]*(s-2)%MOD
 ans=fact[K]
 for L in runs:
    ops=(L-1)//2
    ans = ans * df[L] % MOD
    ans = ans * invfact[ops] % MOD
 print(ans)

Implementation plan:
def main():
    import sys
    MOD=998244353
    data=list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N=data[0]
    A=data[1:1+N]
    if A[0]!=1 or A[-1]!=N%2:
        print(0); return
    runs=[]
    cnt=1
    for i in range(1,N):
        if A[i]==A[i-1]:
            cnt+=1
        else:
            if cnt%2==0:
                print(0); return
            runs.append(cnt)
            cnt=1
    if cnt%2==0:
        print(0); return
    runs.append(cnt)
    M=len(runs)
    K=(N-M)//2  # since all odd, integer
    # But K also sum ops. Could compute sum((L-1)//2 for L in runs) to avoid trust? Use K=sum.
    K=sum((L-1)//2 for L in runs)
    maxL=max(runs)
    # precompute fact/invfact up to K
    fact=[1]*(K+1)
    for i in range(1,K+1):
        fact[i]=fact[i-1]*i%MOD
    invfact=[1]*(K+1)
    invfact[K]=pow(fact[K], MOD-2, MOD)
    for i in range(K,0,-1):
        invfact[i-1]=invfact[i]*i%MOD
    # precompute df up to maxL
    df=[1]*(maxL+1)  # df[1]=1; for odd s set
    for s in range(3,maxL+1,2):
        df[s]=df[s-2]*(s-2)%MOD
    ans=fact[K]
    for L in runs:
        ops=(L-1)//2
        ans = ans * df[L] % MOD
        ans = ans * invfact[ops] % MOD
    print(ans)
Need be careful if maxL=1, df length2, loop range(3,2,2) empty, df[1]=1. If maxL=0 impossible. Good.

Now, let's think about proof of f(s)=(s-2)!! in more detail for our own confidence:
Define F(m) for odd m as number of sequences of operations reducing a valid alternating run sequence of m runs to 1 run, where operations are choosing any triple of consecutive runs. Since lengths irrelevant, F(m) depends only on m. For m=1, F(1)=1. For m>1, the first operation can choose any of m-2 triples. After that, there are m-2 runs, and the remaining process is exactly F(m-2). Thus F(m)=(m-2)F(m-2). Therefore F(2n+1)=(2n-1)!!. This is even simpler than last operation recurrence. Let's verify "remaining process is exactly F(m-2)" despite one run length3: The operation choices in a run sequence are just positions of triples; lengths don't affect validity because colors alternate and any triple has outer same. The number of possible sequences from m-2 runs to 1 run is F(m-2) by definition, regardless of lengths. So yes. This direct recurrence also shows f[L] formula.

Let n=(L-1)/2. df=(2n-1)!! = (2n)!/(2^n n!) = (L-1)!/(2^n n!). Since L-1=2n. So df[L] = fact[L-1] * invfact[n] * inv2^n mod. But iterative df simpler. If we precompute fact up to N, could compute df on fly. But no need.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with final A with multiple blocks and interleaving: Suppose block A has f[L_A] sequences, block B f[L_B]. Total operations K_A+K_B. Number of interleavings = C(K, K_A) = K!/(K_A!K_B!). This is exactly fact[K]*invfact[K_A]*invfact[K_B]. For multiple blocks, multinomial. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any possibility that operations in different blocks are not independent because the run count of the whole sequence affects which triples are available? But operations in a block only depend on runs within that block. The global run sequence is concatenation; an operation inside block k chooses a triple of consecutive runs within block k. The positions of those runs in the global sequence shift due to operations in previous blocks? Actually if operations in previous blocks reduce run counts, the indices of runs in block k shift, but the relative order and the triple choices within block k are unaffected. The actual l,r cell indices also shift? Wait, cell indices are fixed; operations in previous blocks change lengths of runs in previous blocks, which changes the starting cell index of block k. But the (l,r) for an operation inside block k depends on cell boundaries, which are determined by lengths of all previous blocks. If operations in previous blocks occur before, the boundaries shift. However, when we count sequences as interleavings, the actual (l,r) values at each time are determined by the state, including previous operations. Does the same block-internal sequence yield different (l,r) values depending on interleaving? Yes, but the sequence is still valid. We count sequences of operations, not just block-internal choices independent of cell indices. Is the number of interleavings still K! / ∏ ops_k!? Need be careful: The operations in different blocks are not completely independent in terms of cell indices because the positions of block k shift when block j operations change lengths of earlier blocks. But an operation inside block k is defined by choosing l,r within block k; its actual l,r values depend on the current lengths of earlier blocks. However, for a fixed block-internal sequence (which triple of runs within block k at each step), the actual l,r at the time it is performed will be determined by the current state, which depends on how many operations in earlier blocks have been performed (because they change lengths of earlier blocks and thus shift block k). Does this affect validity? No, because the operation only cares about cells inside block k; shifting doesn't change relative positions. But when comparing sequences, the (l,r) values might differ for the same block-internal operation if performed at different times due to different interleavings. Is that accounted for? Let's examine.

Suppose two blocks: block1 (cells1-3) and block2 (cells4-6), each length3, final [3,3]. Block1 operation: merge runs (1,2,3) -> l=1,r=3. Block2 operation: merge runs (4,5,6) -> l=4,r=6. These l,r values are fixed and independent. Interleavings: sequence A: op1 (1,3), op2 (4,6). Sequence B: op2 (4,6), op1 (1,3). l,r same. Good.

Now consider block1 length5 (ops two), block2 length3 (ops one). Block1 first operation could merge triple (1,2,3) -> l=1,r=3, or (2,3,4) -> l=2,r=4, or (3,4,5) -> l=3,r=5. Block2 operation merge (6,7,8) maybe l=6,r=8. If block2 operation is performed before block1 operations, block2 length changes from 3 to1, but block1 cell indices unaffected because block2 is after block1. If block1 operation performed before block2, block1 length changes from5 to? Actually merging inside block1 reduces run count but total length of block1 remains5; the cell indices of block2 start at6, unchanged. So l,r for block2 unaffected. In general, operations in a block do not change the total length of that block, only internal run structure. Thus the starting cell index of subsequent blocks is determined by total lengths of previous blocks, which are fixed final block lengths and never change. Wait initial total length of block k is L_k, and operations inside block k preserve total length L_k. Operations in previous blocks also preserve their total lengths. Therefore the cell interval occupied by each final block is fixed from the start! Is that true? Final blocks are intervals of original cells; operations inside a block merge runs but never change the total number of cells in that block. Operations cannot cross boundaries, so the set of cells in each block is invariant. Thus the starting cell index of block k is always 1+sum_{j<k} L_j, independent of operations. So actual l,r for an operation inside block k depend only on internal run boundaries, which evolve according to the block-internal sequence and are not affected by other blocks. Therefore block operations are completely independent with fixed cell coordinates. Good. This reinforces interleaving count.

Wait, is the set of cells in each final block invariant from the start? Yes, because final boundaries are never crossed and operations don't move cells. The final block k consists of a contiguous interval of original cells; operations within that interval merge runs but keep the interval. So the cell interval is fixed. Good. Thus l,r values for operations in block k are determined solely by the internal state of block k, not by other blocks. So interleaving is straightforward.

Now, let's think about if final block intervals are determined by final A run lengths; yes. The first final run length L1 means cells1..L1 final run. Could an operation inside block1 ever include cell L1+1? No, that would cross final boundary. So block1 interval fixed. Good.

. But before final, let's re-evaluate if there is any hidden nuance in "integer i mod 2" maybe they mean `i mod 2` as in i modulo 2, yes.

Let's think if there is any issue with final A where A1=1 and run lengths odd but A_N check redundant; we include.

Let's think if there is any issue with using fact up to K and invfact up to K. Suppose K=0, invfact[0]=pow(1, MOD-2)=1. Good.

Let's think if there is any issue with `df[s] = df[s-2] * (s-2) % MOD` for s=3: df[1]=1, s-2=1 =>1. For s=5: df[3]=1, *3=3. Good.

Let's think if there is any issue with modulo multiplication if ans is int; fine.

Let's think if there is any issue with parsing A if N=1 and data length maybe 2; A=data[1:2] ok.

Let's think if there is any issue with `A[-1] != N % 2` if N=1 and A=[1], ok.

Let's think if there is any issue with final A with A1=1 and odd runs but A_N check redundant; no harm.

Let's think if there is any issue with "l+1 < r" and our triple merge requires middle run length at least1. Since all runs positive, yes.

Let's think if there is any issue with operation choosing a triple of consecutive runs where the middle run is not "internal" because it's first or last? Triple of consecutive runs means middle run has left and right neighbors, so internal. Good.

Let's think if there is any issue with final run lengths odd but M maybe 0? no.

Let's think if there is any issue with answer formula if runs list empty? N>=1, runs not empty.

Let's maybe write the final code now in mind:

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:1+N]

    if A[0] != 1 or A[-1] != N % 2:
        print(0)
        return

    runs = []
    cnt = 1
    for i in range(1, N):
        if A[i] == A[i-1]:
            cnt += 1
        else:
            if cnt % 2 == 0:
                print(0)
                return
            runs.append(cnt)
            cnt = 1
    if cnt % 2 == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    runs.append(cnt)

    K = 0
    maxL = 0
    for L in runs:
        K += (L - 1) // 2
        if L > maxL:
            maxL = L

    fact = [1] * (K + 1)
    for i in range(1, K + 1):
        fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD

    invfact = [1] * (K + 1)
    invfact[K] = pow(fact[K], MOD - 2, MOD)
    for i in range(K, 0, -1):
        invfact[i-1] = invfact[i] * i % MOD

    df = [1] * (maxL + 1)
    for s in range(3, maxL + 1, 2):
        df[s] = df[s-2] * (s - 2) % MOD

    ans = fact[K]
    for L in runs:
        ops = (L - 1) // 2
        ans = ans * df[L] % MOD
        ans = ans * invfact[ops] % MOD

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
